## Reasoning

The champion is a single-state Stop machine (score ~8939). This works because the score is MINIMISED, and since the floor is uniformly gray, robots that are always stopped avoid the phase-2 penalty (moving costs +1/step/robot). But phase-1 also costs +1/step/robot for stationary robots! So the champion is paying ~500 steps × 18 robots = ~9000 points just in phase 1.

The ideal FSM: move during phase 1, then stop when the green block appears. The green wall block signal should be detected via C=7 (perceive color) with color=2 (green).

**Key insight**: Color 2 = green. When the green wall appears, robots using GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2) or perceiving green (C=7, l=2) should detect it.

**Strategy**: 
- State 0: Exploration (moving) 
- State 1: Stop
- Transition from 0→1: C=7 (perceive green, l=2) with high probability

This should score ~0 in phase 1 (robots moving) and ~0 in phase 2 (robots stopped after seeing green).

However, there's a complication: not all robots may see the green wall immediately. I'll add a spreading mechanism using LED colors so robots that see green can signal others.

Let me design the machines:

1. **Champion (elitism)**: Copy verbatim
2. **Core design**: 2-state: Explore → Stop on green detection
3. **Variants**: Different transition probabilities, additional conditions, 3-state versions

For the 2-state FSM:
- State 0: Exploration (B=0), transition to Stop when C=7 (green, l=2) fires
- State 1: Stop (B=1), possibly transition back if no green (but better to stay)

For spreading: A robot that sees green stops and lights up (e.g., cyan LED). Others see cyan via C=7 and also stop.

## Final Answer

**Reasoning:**

The champion (always-stop, score ~8939) pays ~9000 pts in phase 1. The ideal strategy is: move freely during phase 1, then stop when the green wall appears (C=7, color=2). This should score near 0 for phase 1 and near 0 for phase 2. I'll build mostly 2-state FSMs (explore → stop on green) with variations in turning rate and probability, plus some 3-state variants adding a spreading mechanism via LED colors.

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 6

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 50 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 50 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 1.0 --l0x0 2 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion uses 2 states: Exploration (rwm=30, yellow LED) with two transitions to Stop on green (p=0.95) or cyan (p=0.8), and Stop (cyan LED) with tiny prob to return. Score: 4325.

Key insights from round 2:
- Two transitions from explore state (detect green + detect cyan from stopped robots) works better than one
- rwm=30 (slow turning) beats rwm=50 and rwm=70
- The spreading mechanism (stopped robots emit cyan, moving robots detect cyan) is effective
- Single transition (no spreading) scores ~11125 - much worse
- High return probability from stop (0.5) is terrible

**Exploitation**: Vary rwm (10, 20, 30, 40), vary green detection probability (0.95→1.0, 0.9→0.95), vary cyan detection probability (0.8→0.9), try different spreading colors (magenta=5), adjust yellow LED.

**Exploration**: 3-state with GoToColor green as intermediate, 4-state, try adding neighbor-count conditions, try repulsion for better coverage during phase 1.

Note on errors from last round: must always include `--n<I>`, `--n<I>x<K>` destination, valid LED colors (0,4,5,6), valid `--l` values (1-6).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion uses Repulsion (B=5, rep=2.0) with yellow LED, transitioning to Stop on green (p=0.95) or cyan (p=0.8). Score: 1043. This is excellent - repulsion keeps robots spread out and moving, and the dual green/cyan detection stops them efficiently.

Key observations:
- Repulsion (B=5) beats Exploration (B=0) for phase 1 - keeps robots moving and spread
- Cyan LED on stopped robots + cyan detection from movers = spreading mechanism works
- Yellow LED (cle=4) on mover state is important (worst performers used magenta=5 or yellow on stop)
- Stop state must use cyan LED (cle=6) for spreading to work
- The stop state returning with p=0.001 is fine (essentially never returns)
- Detecting yellow (l=4) as third condition was worst - avoid

**Exploitation** (fine-tuning champion):
- Vary rep gain: 1.5, 2.5, 3.0, 1.0
- Vary green detection prob: 0.95→1.0, 0.9
- Vary cyan detection prob: 0.8→0.9, 0.7
- Try p1x0=0.0001 (essentially never return from stop)
- Try adding a third transition from moving state

**Exploration**:
- 3-state: repulsion → GoToColor green → stop
- 4-state variant
- Attraction variant (spreading toward stopped robots)
- Try rep=3.0, rep=4.0

Important rules to remember:
- LED colors: only 0, 4, 5, 6 allowed
- l values: 1-6 only
- Every state needs --n<I>, every transition needs destination
- Repulsion: --rep<I> real in [1..5]

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion uses Repulsion (rep=4.0) with yellow LED, two transitions to Stop on green (p=0.95) or cyan (p=0.8). Score: 969.

Key observations:
- Higher repulsion (4.0) beats lower (2.0, 3.0) - robots spread better and move more
- rep=5.0 (maximum) should be tried
- The dual green+cyan detection with p=0.95 and p=0.8 is the sweet spot
- Three transitions from moving state slightly worse (score 1037 vs 969)
- Lower cyan detection prob (0.7) is worse; higher (0.9) slightly worse too - 0.8 is good
- p1x0=0.0001 (essentially never return) slightly better than 0.001
- Attraction (att) is terrible for phase 1

**Exploitation**: 
- Try rep=5.0 (max), rep=4.5, rep=3.5
- Try p1x0=0.0 (but must be >0 per rules, so use 0.0001)
- Try green p=1.0 with rep=4.0
- Try cyan p=0.85 with rep=4.0
- Combine rep=4.0 with p1x0=0.0001

**Exploration**:
- 3-state: repulsion → stop (direct from green), with GoToColor as intermediate
- Try rep=5 with different transition probs
- Try adding neighbor condition as additional trigger

## Reasoning

The champion uses Repulsion (rep=4.0, yellow LED), transitions to Stop on green (p=0.95) or cyan (p=0.8), with return probability 0.0001. Score: 927.85.

Key observations from round 5:
- p1x0=0.0001 is better than 0.001 (champion vs rank 2-5)
- rep=4.0 beats rep=5.0 with 0.0001 return prob; but rep=5.0 with 0.0001 is second best
- cyan detection p=0.8 is better than 0.85, 0.9 (with p1x0=0.001)
- green detection p=0.95 is the sweet spot

**Exploitation**:
- Try rep=4.0, p1x0=0.0001 with cyan p=0.85, 0.9, 0.75
- Try rep=5.0, p1x0=0.0001 with cyan p=0.85, 0.9
- Try rep=4.5, p1x0=0.0001
- Try green p=1.0 with p1x0=0.0001
- Try p1x0=0.00001 (essentially zero)

**Exploration**:
- 3-state with rep → GoToColor green → stop (with p1x0=0.0001)
- Add neighbor-below condition as extra trigger to stop (if isolated after green appears)

All machines must have correct syntax - every state needs --n<I>, every transition needs destination, valid LED colors (0,4,5,6), valid l values (1-6).

- rep=4.0-4.5 range more finely
- cyan prob: 0.78, 0.82, 0.80 (stay near 0.8)
- green prob: 0.95 seems fixed
- Combine rep=5 with cyan=0.8, p1x0=0.0001
- Try rep=4 with p1x0=0.00005

For exploration, try:
- GoAwayColor green (B=9) as moving state - robots move away from green wall during phase 1, then stop when seeing green
- Different spreading colors (magenta=5 instead of cyan=6) but keep yellow on moving state

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The new champion is dramatically better: score 237.05! It uses **GoAwayColor green (B=9)** during phase 1 - robots actively flee the green wall area. When the green wall appears, they're already oriented away from it but still moving; then they detect green (C=7, l=2) or cyan from stopped robots (C=7, l=6) and stop. This is brilliant: during phase 1 there's no green wall, so robots just move around; when green appears, robots both directly detect it AND get the spreading signal from stopped neighbors.

Key insight: GoAwayColor green (B=9) during phase 1 means robots are constantly moving (avoiding the non-existent green), and when green appears they immediately see it and stop.

**Exploitation** (fine-tune champion):
- Vary green detection prob: 0.9, 1.0, 0.97
- Vary cyan detection prob: 0.75, 0.82, 0.85, 0.9
- Vary p1x0: 0.00001, 0.00005
- Keep vel=1.0, clr=2 (green), cle=4 (yellow), cle1=6 (cyan)

**Exploration**:
- GoAwayColor with color=1 (red) - different color to flee
- 3-state: GoAwayColor → GoToColor green → Stop
- GoAwayColor green with magenta spreading (cle=5, l=5) - but we learned magenta is bad
- Try GoToColor green (B=8) as alternative but with better params
- Add neighbor condition as additional stop trigger

## Reasoning

The champion (score 215.17) holds with GoAwayColor green (B=9), yellow LED, green detection p=0.95, cyan detection p=0.9, return p=0.0001.

This round's best performers scored ~285-300, all worse than the champion. The champion wasn't beaten. Key findings:
- p1x0=0 (hard zero return) gives 285.26 - worse than 0.0001
- cyan p=1.0 gives 286.13 - close but worse than 0.9
- cyan p=0.95 gives 299 - worse
- cyan p=0.88 gives 301.76 - worse (0.9 is sweet spot)
- p1x0=0.0002 gives 303.71 - worse than 0.0001
- GoAwayColor clr=1 (red) gives 300.63 - worse than clr=2 (green)

The champion parameters seem well-tuned. For the final round, I should:
1. Keep the champion (elitism)
2. Try very fine variations around the champion's exact params
3. Explore a few genuinely different structures

Fine variations to try:
- green p: 0.94, 0.96, 0.95 (already best)
- cyan p: 0.89, 0.91, 0.90 (already best)  
- p1x0: 0.0001 (already best), try 0.00008, 0.00015
- Combined: green=0.96, cyan=0.9; green=0.95, cyan=0.91

Exploration:
- 3-state with GoAwayColor → GoToColor → Stop (clean version)
- GoAwayColor with additional neighbor-based stop condition
- Try different LED on stop state (0 instead of 6) - but 6 is needed for spreading

Actually the spreading mechanism is crucial: stopped robots emit cyan (cle1=6), moving robots detect cyan (l0x1=6). This is why cle1=6 and l0x1=6 must match.

Let me try variations carefully:

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. This mission has TWO SUCCESSIVE PHASES separated by a signal, and the scoring rule is REVERSED between them. The score is MINIMISED.

PHASE 1 - BEFORE THE SIGNAL. At the start of each run the loop function draws a secret switch time uniformly between 400 and 600 simulation steps. Until that step, the score gains +1 for every robot that is STATIONARY. During phase 1 the robots must KEEP MOVING.

PHASE 2 - AFTER THE SIGNAL. Exactly at the switch time, one randomly chosen wall block of the arena turns GREEN. From that step on the rule flips: the score gains +1 for every robot that is MOVING. The robots must STOP and stay still for the rest of the run.

A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. Stopping immediately is a BAD strategy: it costs about one point per robot for each of the ~500 steps of phase 1. Moving for the whole run is equally bad, for the ~700 steps of phase 2. The machine needs at least one moving state and one stopped state, and the transition into the stopped state must be driven by PERCEIVING THE GREEN BLOCK. A transition based only on a fixed probability cannot be accurate, because the switch time is redrawn at every run. The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor conditions carry no information in this mission.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 8 walls, inner radius 0.88 m. 18 e-puck robots start scattered inside a disc of radius 0.62 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.58 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots, an inner radius of 0.91 m and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
